P70 [align=LEFT (0), style=Body Text]: 
P71 [align=JUSTIFY (3), style=Normal]: The complexity and diversity of human diseases have long presented challenges for the profession of medical diagnosis. By utilizing vast volumes of medical data, Artificial Intelligence approaches have made it possible to automate and optimize the diagnosing process. Regarding the field of ophthalmology, early detection and accurate diagnosis of eye diseases are critical to ensuing timely treatment and preventing vision loss. However, differentiating between these eye diseases can be challenging task. The objective is to create a deep learning model that can precisely identify three types of eye diseases: cataracts, glaucoma and diabetic retinopathy - and a no-disease eye - normal eye - based on a dataset of approximately 1000 images of human retinal for each class.

P94 [align=LEFT (0), style=Body Text]: 
P95 [align=None, style=Body Text]: The human eye is a remarkably complex organ, and diseases affecting it can have profound impacts on an individual’s quality of life. Among the numerous ophthalmic conditions, cataracts, glaucoma, and diabetic retinopathy are the most prevalent causes of visual impairment and blindness globally. According to the World Health Organization (WHO), hundreds of millions of people suffer from visual deficiencies, with many of these cases being preventable or treatable if diagnosed early. The burden on healthcare systems is growing rapidly, especially in low-resource settings where access to trained ophthalmologists is limited. This highlights the urgent need for efficient, accessible, and accurate diagnostic solutions.

P96 [align=None, style=Body Text]: Traditional diagnostic methods for eye diseases rely heavily on manual inspection of retinal fundus images, a process that is not only labour-intensive but also prone to variability between clinicians. The interpretation of retinal images requires significant expertise, and even experienced practitioners may face challenges in differentiating between subtle patterns and overlapping symptoms of various diseases. This variability contributes to misdiagnosis and delays in treatment, ultimately risking irreversible vision damage.

P97 [align=None, style=Body Text]: With the advent of Artificial Intelligence (AI) and, more specifically, Deep Learning (DL), the medical field has witnessed a paradigm shift. Deep learning models, particularly Convolutional Neural Networks (CNNs), have demonstrated remarkable success in image classification tasks across a variety of domains, including medical imaging. These models are capable of learning intricate patterns and hierarchical features directly from raw image data, eliminating the need for hand-crafted features that often limit traditional machine learning approaches.

P113 [align=None, style=Body Text]: This paper discusses the use of artificial intelligence to create a model for identifying eye diseases in their early stages using fundus images. P116 [align=None, style=Body Text]: This paper introduces a fusion-based framework utilizing pre-trained Convolutional Neural Network (CNN) models, such as Inception v3, ResNet, and DenseNet121, to improve the identification and classification of diabetic retinopathy. By integrating these CNN models with machine learning classifiers like Support Vector Machine (SVM) and Random Forest, their system showed substantial enhancements in accuracy. This fusion strategy leverages the advantages of each model, as Inception v3, ResNet, and DenseNet121 are recognized for their proficiency in capturing nuanced characteristics in complicated images. The research highlights the advantages of combining various deep learning architectures to improve diagnosis accuracy, especially in medical imaging where little differences are critical markers of illness severity. This method underscores an effective strategy in AI-based diagnostics by enhancing detection rates, hence fostering more precise and dependable automated systems for diabetic retinopathy evaluation.

P194 [align=None, style=Body Text]: Following the introduction, a detailed review of related literature is provided. This section critically analyses previous work in the field of medical image analysis, particularly focusing on the use of machine learning and deep learning techniques for ophthalmic disease detection. The literature review highlights current methodologies, datasets, and model architectures, identifying both their contributions and existing limitations that this project aims to overcome. The methodology chapter outlines the research design and technical approach adopted to achieve the study's objectives. It details the dataset selection, preprocessing techniques, deep learning model architectures used such as CNNs, DenseNet121, SVM and Xception, and the training and evaluation procedures. This section ensures that the research process is transparent, systematic, and reproducible.

P204 [align=JUSTIFY (3), style=Heading 5]: Medical Image Classification
   Runs: ['Medical', bold=None, italic=None, font=None, size=None] | [' ', bold=None, italic=None, font=None, size=None] | ['Image', bold=None, italic=None, font=None, size=None] | [' ', bold=None, italic=None, font=None, size=None] | ['Classification', bold=None, italic=None, font=None, size=None]
P205 [align=None, style=Body Text]: Medical image classification involves assigning diagnostic labels to visual medical data, such as retinal fundus images. In ophthalmology, accurate image classification is vital for the early detection of diseases like cataract, glaucoma, and diabetic retinopathy, as well as for distinguishing these from a normal (healthy) eye. The objective is to reduce diagnostic error and aid in clinical decision-making through automation.

P208 [align=None, style=Body Text]: Artificial Intelligence (AI), particularly in the form of Machine Learning (ML) and Deep Learning (DL), has revolutionized healthcare by enabling machines to learn patterns from data and make accurate predictions. Supervised learning algorithms such as Random Forest Classifier and Support Vector Machine (SVM) are frequently used for structured classification tasks. These methods are capable of analysing labelled image features and forming decision boundaries for classification.

P251 [align=LEFT (0), style=Body Text]: 
P252 [align=None, style=Body Text]: This project aims to develop a deep learning-based model capable of accurately classifying three common eye diseases—cataracts, glaucoma, and diabetic retinopathy—as well as identifying normal (healthy) eyes. The model is trained on a dataset consisting of approximately 1000 retinal images per class.
   Runs: ['This project aims to develop a deep learning-based model capable of accurately classifying three common eye diseases—cataracts, glaucoma, and diabetic retinopathy—as well as identifying normal (healthy) eyes. Through this approach, the project seeks to enhance the efficiency and accuracy of ophthalmic diagnostics by leveraging AI and modern image processing techniques in response to the growing incidence of eye diseases.

P285 [align=None, style=Body Text]: Model Design and Training: Multiple machine learning and deep learning models are trained: Convolutional Neural Networks (CNN), Random Forest, Support Vector Machine (SVM) classifier, Xception, DenseNet121. Transfer learning is used for Xception and DenseNet121 by loading pre-trained ImageNet weights and fine-tuning them on the eye disease dataset. Models are trained using a train-test-split. Loss functions like categorical cross-entropy and optimizers like Adam are used for training.

P291 [align=LEFT (0), style=Body Text]: 
P292 [align=JUSTIFY (3), style=Heading 5]: Convolutional Neural Network (CNN)
   Runs: ['Convolutional', bold=None, italic=None, font=None, size=None] | [' ', bold=None, italic=None, font=None, size=None] | ['Neural', bold=None, italic=None, font=None, size=None] | [' ', bold=None, italic=None, font=None, size=None] | ['Network', bold=None, italic=None, font=None, size=None] | [' ', bold=None, italic=None, font=None, size=None] | ['(CNN)', bold=None, italic=None, font=None, size=None]
P293 [align=None, style=Body Text]: This CNN model was developed using the Keras API within the TensorFlow framework, and trained on images. The input shape of the images was standardized to 224×224 pixels with 3 color channels (RGB). The model began with a Conv2D layer of 16 filters with a 3×3 size, ReLU activation followed by the MaxPooling2D layer after first Conv2d layer. This initial block allowed the model to learn basic spatial features. Deeper convolutional layers followed, including 32 filters with 3×3 size, ReLU activations for better convergence and generalization, followed by the MaxPooling2D layer after second Conv2d layer. Again, another set of 64 filters with 3x3 size included followed by MaxPooling2D layer. As you go deeper, the model learns more complex and abstract features. Then the 2D feature map is converted into 1D vector so it can be passed to dense layer with 128 neurons with ReLu activation. Finally, the output layer consisted of a dense layer with a number of neurons equal to the number of classes (4), and a softmax activation function was used to generate a probability distribution over the classes. The model was compiled using the ‘adam’ optimizer.

   Runs: ['DenseNet121:', bold=None, italic=None, font=None, size=None]
P295 [align=None, style=Body Text]: This DenseNet121 model was developed using the Keras API with transfer learning. It leverages pretrained weights, custom dense layers, and F1-score monitoring, making it suitable for eye disease classification.
   Runs: ['This', bold=None, italic=None, font=None, size=None] | [' ', bold=None, italic=None, font=None, size=None] | ['DenseNet121 model', bold=None, italic=None, font=None, size=None] | [' ', bold=None, italic=None, font=None, size=None] | ['was', bold=None, italic=None, font=None, size=None] | [' ', bold=None, italic=None, font=None, size=None] | ['developed using the Keras API with', bold=None, italic=None, font=None, size=None] | [' ', bold=None, italic=None, font=None, size=None] | ['transfer learning. P296 [align=None, style=Body Text]: It begins by loading a pretrained DenseNet121 model (excluding the top layers) with ImageNet weights and freezing its layers to retain learned features. A custom classification head is added, consisting of multiple dense layers (512, 256, 128, 64 units) with ReLU activation, followed by a softmax output layer for 4-class

This approach enables the powerful Xception model to be adapted effectively for eye disease classification.

   Runs: ['Support Vector', bold=None, italic=None, font=None, size=None] | [' ', bold=None, italic=None, font=None, size=None] | ['Machine', bold=None, italic=None, font=None, size=None] | [' ', bold=None, italic=None, font=None, size=None] | ['(SVM)', bold=None, italic=None, font=None, size=None] | [' ', bold=None, italic=None, font=None, size=None] | ['Classifier:', bold=None, italic=None, font=None, size=None]
P311 [align=None, style=Body Text]: The model was built using Scikit-learn library, which is a popular Python package for machine learning. It starts by reading images from a directory where each subfolder corresponds to an eye disease label (e.g., glaucoma, cataract, normal, diabetic retinopathy). Each image is converted to grayscale, resized to 128×128 pixels, and normalized to ensure uniform input. The labels are extracted from folder names. The dataset is then split into training and testing sets (80/20), and the image data is flattened into 1D arrays to match the input format required by SVM models. An SVM classifier is created using a linear kernel and trained on the flattened training images. The model is configured with probability=True to allow for probability-based predictions. This setup leverages Scikit-learn’s SVM implementation for performing multi-class eye disease classification based on grayscale image features.

P359 [align=None, style=Body Text]: Deep learning models like Xception and DenseNet121 performed the best in terms of accuracy. They were able to recognize small and complex features in the eye images, making them very effective in detecting different eye conditions. However, these models required more computation and took longer to train.

P360 [align=None, style=Body Text]: Traditional machine learning models like Random Forest and SVM were quicker and easier to interpret. They worked well on simpler cases but were not as accurate when dealing with more detailed or overlapping eye disease patterns.

P367 [align=JUSTIFY (3), style=Heading 4]: Results of CNN Model
   Runs: ['', bold=False, italic=None, font=None, size=None] | ['Results', bold=None, italic=None, font=None, size=None] | [' ', bold=None, italic=None, font=None, size=None] | ['of', bold=None, italic=None, font=None, size=None] | [' ', bold=None, italic=None, font=None, size=None] | ['CNN', bold=None, italic=None, font=None, size=None] | [' ', bold=None, italic=None, font=None, size=None] | ['Model', bold=None, italic=None, font=None, size=None]
P368 [align=None, style=Body Text]: Table 7.1.1 displays classification report of the CNN model, detailing performance metrics across four vulnerability classes. The overall accuracy achieved by the model is 86% on a dataset of 843 samples. Class 1 (Cataract) performs the best with a precision of 0.85, a near-perfect recall of 0.90, and the F1-score of 0.88, indicating the model is highly reliable in identifying this class. Class 2 (diabetic retinopathy) also has stronger precision of 0.99, though its recall of 0.98 and the F1-score to 0.99. Class 3 (Glaucoma) also has precision 0.73, though its recall of 0.79 and the F1-score to 0.76. Class 4 (Normal) has precision 0.84, though its recall of 0.74 and the F1-score to 0.79. The CNN model performed best in identifying diabetic retinopathy, followed by cataract. Overall, the CNN model achieved a solid accuracy of 86%, indicating its effectiveness for eye disease classification.

   Runs: ['', bold=False, italic=None, font=None, size=None] | ['Result', bold=None, italic=None, font=None, size=None] | [' ', bold=None, italic=None, font=None, size=None] | ['of', bold=None, italic=None, font=None, size=None] | [' ', bold=None, italic=None, font=None, size=None] | ['DenseNet121', bold=None, italic=None, font=None, size=None] | [' ', bold=None, italic=None, font=None, size=None] | ['Model', bold=None, italic=None, font=None, size=None]
P374 [align=None, style=Body Text]: Figure 7.2.1 shows the Model accuracy and Model Loss plots. The given plots describe that DenseNet121 shows excellent performance in both accuracy and loss metrics. The model achieves high accuracy (above 90%) for both training and validation, and the loss values remain low, implying robust learning without major overfitting. Due to DenseNet121 model’s high classification accuracy, stable validation performance, and efficient learning behaviour, DenseNet121 proves to be a highly reliable and effective model. Its ability to handle complex medical image data with consistency makes it an ideal candidate for real-world deployment in automated eye disease screening systems, where precision and reliability are critical.

P379 [align=None, style=Body Text]: The graph in given Figure 7.3.1 illustrates the performance of the Xception model during training and validation for eye disease classification. The model shows excellent training accuracy, reaching above 95%, and maintains low training loss, indicating effective learning. However, the validation accuracy fluctuates significantly across epochs, and the validation loss shows instability with sharp spikes, suggesting potential overfitting or sensitivity to the validation data. Despite these fluctuations, the validation performance improves toward the end, making the Xception model a promising option with further tuning for better generalization.

P383 [align=LEFT (0), style=Body Text]: 
P384 [align=None, style=Body Text]: Table 7.4.1 displays the classification report for the Random Forest model used in eye disease classification. The model achieved an overall accuracy of 71% on the test dataset of 584 samples. Among the three classes: Glaucoma had a precision of 0.75, but a lower recall of 0.54, resulting in an F1-score of 0.63, indicating that many glaucoma cases were missed. Cataract achieved a precision of 0.68 and a recall of 0.63, giving an F1-score of 0.65, which suggests moderate performance. Normal cases had the highest recall at 0.92, meaning most normal cases were correctly identified, but the precision was 0.70, leading to an F1-score of 0.80.

P392 [align=None, style=Body Text]: The Figure 7.5.1 shows the pie chart that displays the data distribution for the dataset used in training the Support Vector Machine (SVM) model for eye disease classification. The dataset is nearly evenly distributed among the four classes: Diabetic Retinopathy: 26.0%, Normal: 25.5%, Cataract: 24.6%, Glaucoma: 23.9%. Such balanced distribution ensures that the SVM model receives a fair representation of each class, which is essential for learning accurate decision boundaries and minimizing bias toward any specific eye disease category during classification.

P393 [align=None, style=Body Text]: The Figure 7.5.2 presents a 2D visualization of the SVM model's decision boundaries using bright colors to represent four eye disease classes: glaucoma, cataract, normal, and diabetic retinopathy. The data points, likely reduced via dimensionality reduction techniques. show that normal cases are relatively well-clustered, while cataract, glaucoma, and diabetic retinopathy samples exhibit considerable overlap. This overlap suggests that the SVM model faces challenges in clearly distinguishing between certain classes, particularly in lower-dimensional space. Overall, the visualization highlights the complexity of multi-class classification in medical imaging and the limitations of SVM in handling overlapping class distributions.

P394 [align=None, style=Body Text]: 
P395 [align=None, style=Body Text]: Table 7.5.1 shows the classification report for an SVM (Support Vector Machine) model applied to an eye disease dataset with four classes: glaucoma, cataract, normal, and diabetic retinopathy. The model achieved the highest F1-score of 0.74 for the "normal" class, indicating strong performance in identifying healthy cases. Cataract and glaucoma achieved moderate F1-scores of 0.68 and 0.62, respectively, showing acceptable but weaker performance. Notably, the diabetic retinopathy class shows perfect scores (precision, recall, F1-score = 1.00), but with zero support, meaning there were no samples of this class in the test set—making the metric statistically invalid. Overall, the model attained a micro average F1-score of 0.68 and a macro average of 0.76

P403 [align=LEFT (0), style=Body Text]: 
P404 [align=None, style=Body Text]: This project successfully developed a deep learning-based classification system for the accurate detection of common eye diseases, including Diabetic Retinopathy, Cataract, Glaucoma, and Normal (healthy) cases, using retinal fundus images. The system integrates multiple machine learning and deep learning models such as Random Forest, SVM, CNN, DenseNet121, and Xception to compare and evaluate classification performance.
   Runs: ['This project successfully developed a deep learning-based classification system for the accurate detection of common eye diseases, including Diabetic Retinopathy, Cataract, Glaucoma, and Normal (healthy) cases, using retinal fundus images. P405 [align=None, style=Body Text]: A comprehensive approach was adopted, beginning with image acquisition and preprocessing, where techniques like resizing, normalization, and enhancement were used to ensure high-quality, model-ready input. These processed images were then used to train various models, with a focus on deep learning architectures that can extract complex visual patterns associated with eye conditions.

P406 [align=None, style=Body Text]: The evaluation phase revealed that CNN-based models with transfer learning (DenseNet121 and Xception) achieved superior performance in terms of accuracy, generalization, and reliability, making them ideal for deployment in real-world healthcare applications. While traditional models like Random Forest and SVM performed adequately on simpler patterns, they were less effective for the fine detection required in medical imagery.

P414 [align=None, style=Body Text]: The proposed system "Comprehensive Eye Disease Classification Using Deep Learning" presents an effective and automated approach to detecting and classifying common eye diseases such as Diabetic Retinopathy, Cataract, Glaucoma, and Normal (healthy) cases using retinal fundus images. With the increasing prevalence of vision-related disorders and the growing demand for early and accurate diagnosis, this system offers a valuable tool to support ophthalmologists and healthcare providers.

P415 [align=None, style=Body Text]: By leveraging the power of Convolutional Neural Networks (CNNs) and transfer learning models like DenseNet121 and Xception, the system is able to achieve high classification accuracy. The project also explored traditional machine learning algorithms such as Random Forest and SVM for comparative analysis. Image preprocessing techniques—including resizing, normalization, and contrast enhancement—ensured that the input data is standardized and suitable for training.

P416 [align=None, style=Body Text]: Through extensive training, testing, and evaluation using various performance metrics such as accuracy, precision, recall, and F1-score, the deep learning-based models demonstrated superior ability to distinguish between different eye diseases. The classification results showed that transfer learning models like DenseNet121 and Xception, significantly outperform conventional machine learning methods in medical image analysis tasks.
